How answers are graded:
How answers are graded

- Each attempt is compared with your answer and marked right or wrong. The grade is on the whole attempt; the final answer shown is picked out automatically as a guide.
- Numbers match when they are the same value written differently: 0.675, 67.5% and 67.5 percentage points all match, and so do different scales (thousand, million, billion), thousands separators, currency symbols and numbers written as words.
- If the question asks for a particular format (a percentage, a number of decimal places, a unit, a rounding or an order), the attempt must meet it. If it asks for none, any equivalent form is accepted.
- For an ordered list, a title, honorific or article added to an entry is accepted if the order is right either with it or without it.
- Only the value the attempt finally commits to counts. Values reached while working, or considered and set aside, do not. An attempt that offers several values without choosing one is wrong, even if one of them is right.

Full answer
-----------

Looking at this problem, I need to determine two dates:

1. The first statement deadline under the Commission's proposal
2. The end of the first accounting year the adopted directive's statement must cover

First statement deadline under the proposal

If the proposal had entered into force on 25 July 2024 (same date as the adopted directive):

- Article 30(1)(a): For companies in Article 2(1)(a) (>500 employees and >EUR 150 million turnover), Member States apply provisions from 2 years after entry into force = 25 July 2026
- Article 11: "The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year"

The company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion turnover, easily meeting the Article 2(1)(a) thresholds. Therefore, the first statement deadline is 30 April 2027 (covering calendar year 2026).

First accounting year under the adopted directive

The company meets the directive's Article 2(1)(a) thresholds (>1,000 employees, >EUR 450 million turnover).

However, the staggered application in Article 37(1) depends on size:

- (a) >5,000 employees AND >EUR 1.5 billion → 26 July 2027
- (b) >3,000 employees AND >EUR 900 million → 26 July 2028

The company has 6,000 employees (meets >3,000) and EUR 1.2 billion (meets >EUR 900 million but NOT >EUR 1.5 billion). It falls under category (b).

Application date: 26 July 2028 for substantive obligations. Article 16 (reporting): Applies to "financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029."

The company's financial year runs 1 July to 30 June:

- FY 2028/29: starts 1 July 2028 (before 1 Jan 2029) → not subject to Art. 16
- FY 2029/30: starts 1 July 2029 (after 1 Jan 2029) → first year subject to Art. 16

This financial year ends on 30 June 2030.

Calculation

From 30 April 2027 to 30 June 2030:

- 30 April 2027 → 30 April 2030 = 36 months
- 30 April 2030 → 30 June 2030 = 2 months
- Total = 38 months

Answer

38 months

Reasoning
---------

The user is asking a complex question that requires me to compare the Commission's proposal (Document 1) with the adopted directive (Document 3), specifically regarding the timing of when statements must be published and what accounting year they must cover.

Let me break down the key information:

**Company details:**
- 6,000 employees
- EUR 1.2 billion worldwide turnover
- Accounts closed each 30 June

**Question: How many months after its first statement deadline under the proposal does the first accounting year its directive statement must cover end?**

I need to find:
1. The first statement deadline under the proposal (Document 1)
2. The first accounting year the directive statement must cover under the adopted directive (Document 3)

**Under the Commission's proposal (Document 1):**

Article 11 states: "The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

So under the proposal, the deadline is 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year.

**Under the adopted directive (Document 3):**

Article 16(1)(b) states: "within a reasonable period of time, but no later than 12 months after the balance sheet date of the financial year for which the statement is drawn up, or, for companies voluntarily reporting in accordance with Directive 2013/34/EU, by the date of publication of the annual financial statements."

So the deadline is 12 months after the balance sheet date.

Now, the question is about the first statement deadline under the proposal. Since the proposal is no longer relevant (it was superseded by the adopted directive), I need to interpret this as: if the proposal had entered into force, when would the first statement deadline be?

The proposal was published on 23.2.2022. Under Article 30 of the proposal:
- Member States shall adopt and publish, by [2 years from entry into force] at the latest, regulations and administrative provisions necessary to comply with this Directive.
- They shall apply those provisions as follows:
  - from [2 years from entry into force] as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), point (a), and Article 2(2), point (a)

The company in question has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion turnover, which falls under Article 2(1), point (a) of the proposal (more than 500 employees and more than EUR 150 million turnover).

But wait, the question says "Had the Commission's proposal entered into force when the adopted directive did". The adopted directive entered into force on the twentieth day following its publication in the Official Journal. The adopted directive was published on 5 July 2024, so it entered into force on 25 July 2024.

Actually, let me re-read the proposal's Article 30 more carefully:

"1.Member States shall adopt and publish, by … [OJ to insert: 2 years from the entry into force of this Directive] at the latest, regulations and administrative provisions necessary to comply with this Directive. They shall forthwith communicate to the Commission the text of those provisions.

They shall apply those provisions as follows:
(a) from… [OJ to insert: 2 years from the entry into force of this Directive] as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), point (a), and Article 2(2), point (a);
(b) from … [OJ to insert: 4 years from the entry into force of this Directive] as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), point (b), and Article 2(2), point (b)."

So under the proposal, if it entered into force on the same date as the adopted directive (25 July 2024), then:
- Entry into force: 25 July 2024
- Transposition deadline: 25 July 2026 (2 years)
- Application: from 25 July 2026 for large companies (group 1)

The company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion turnover, which is well above the thresholds in both the proposal and the directive.

Under the proposal, Article 11: "The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

So the first statement deadline under the proposal would be 30 April 2027, covering the calendar year 2026.

Wait, I need to think about this more carefully. The application date is 25 July 2026. The first statement would cover the previous calendar year. So the first statement deadline would be 30 April 2027, covering calendar year 2026.

Actually, looking at the proposal more carefully:

Article 11: "Member States shall ensure that companies that are not subject to reporting requirements under Articles 19a and 29a of Directive 2013/34/EU report on the matters covered by this Directive by publishing on their website an annual statement in a language customary in the sphere of international business. The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

So the deadline is 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year.

Under the adopted directive, Article 16:
"(1) Without prejudice to the exemption provided for in paragraph 2 of this Article, Member States shall ensure that companies report on the matters covered by this Directive by publishing on their website an annual statement. That annual statement shall be published:
...

Reasoning
---------

Let me analyze this question carefully. I need to determine how many months after its first statement deadline under the Commission's proposal the first accounting year ends for a specific EU company under the adopted directive.

First, let me identify the key information about the company:
- Always has 6,000 employees
- EUR 1.2 billion worldwide turnover
- Accounts closed each 30 June (so fiscal year ends June 30)

Now I need to understand:
1. When the Commission's proposal would have required this company to publish its first due diligence statement
2. When the adopted directive requires this company to publish its first due diligence statement
3. The difference in months between these two deadlines, specifically how many months after the proposal's deadline the directive's first accounting year ends

Let me look at the documents to find the relevant information about implementation timelines.

From Document 1 (the Commission's proposal, dated 23.2.2022), I see in Article 30 (Transposition):

"1. Member States shall adopt and publish, by … [OJ to insert: 2 years from the entry into force of this Directive] at the latest, regulations and administrative provisions necessary to comply with this Directive. They shall forthwith communicate to the Commission the text of those provisions.

They shall apply those provisions as follows:

(a) from… [OJ to insert: 2 years from the entry into force of this Directive] as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), point (a), and Article 2(2), point (a);

(b) from … [OJ to insert: 4 years from the entry into force of this Directive] as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), point (b), and Article 2(2), point (b)."

And Article 31: "This Directive shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union."

From Document 3 (the adopted directive, dated 13 June 2024), I see in Article 37 (Transposition):

"1. Member States shall adopt and publish, by 26 July 2026, the laws, regulations and administrative provisions necessary to comply with this Directive. They shall forthwith communicate the text of those measures to the Commission.

They shall apply those measures:

(a) from 26 July 2027 as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), points (a) and (b), which are formed in accordance with the legislation of the Member State and that had more than 5 000 employees on average and generated a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 1 500 000 000 in the last financial year preceding 26 July 2027 for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted, with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2028;

(b) from 26 July 2028 as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), points (a) and (b), which are formed in accordance with the legislation of the Member State and that had more than 3 000 employees on average and generated a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 900 000 000 in the last financial year preceding 26 July 2028 for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted, with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029;

(c) from 26 July 2027 as regards companies referred to in Article 2(2), points (a) and (b), which are formed in accordance with the legislation of a third country and that generated a net turnover of more than EUR 1 500 000 000 in the Union, in the financial year preceding the last financial year preceding 26 July 2027, with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2028;

(d) from 26 July 2028 as regards companies referred to in Article 2(2), points (a) and (b), which are formed in accordance with the legislation of a third country and that generated a net turnover of more than EUR 900 000 000 in the Union, in the financial year preceding the last financial year preceding 26 July 2028, with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029;

(e) from 26 July 2029 as regards all other companies referred to in Article 2(1), points (a) and (b), and Article 2(2), points (a) and (b), and companies referred to in Article 2(1), point (c), and Article 2(2), point (c), with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029."

Also, Article 38: "This Directive shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union."

And from the header of Document 3: "DIRECTIVE (EU) 2024/1760 OF THE EUROPEAN PARLIAMENT AND OF THE COUNCIL of 13 June 2024" and "5.7.2024" which appears to be the publication date in the Official Journal.

Let me check Document 1 again for the scope criteria to see which category our company falls into.

From Document 1, Article 2 (Scope):

"1. This Directive shall apply to companies which are formed in accordance with the legislation of a Member State and which fulfil one of the following conditions:

(a) the company had more than 500 employees on average and had a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 150 million in the last financial year for which annual financial statements have been prepared;

(b) the company did not reach the thresholds under point (a), but had more than 250 employees on average and had a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 40 million in the last financial year for which annual financial statements have been prepared, provided that at least 50% of this net turnover was generated in one or more of the following sectors: [high-impact sectors listed]"

Our company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion turnover, so it clearly falls under Article 2(1)(a) in the proposal.

In the adopted directive (Document 3), Article 2:

"1. This Directive shall apply to companies which are formed in accordance with the legislation of a Member State and which fulfil one of the following conditions:

(a) the company had more than 1 000 employees on average and had a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 450 000 000 in the last financial year for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted;

(b) the company did not reach the thresholds as referred to in point (a) but is the ultimate parent company of a group that reached those thresholds in the last financial year for which consolidated annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted;

(c) the company entered into or is the ultimate parent company of a group that entered into franchising or licensing agreements in the Union in return for royalties with independent third-party companies, where those agreements ensure a common identity, a common business concept and the application of uniform business methods, and where those royalties amounted to more than EUR 22 500 000 in the last financial year for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted, and provided that the company had or is the ultimate parent company of a group that had a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 80 000 000 in the last financial year for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted."

Our company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion turnover, so it falls under Article 2(1)(a) in the adopted directive as well.

Now, let's look at the reporting requirements.

In the proposal (Document 1), Article 11 (Communicating):

"Member States shall ensure that companies that are not subject to reporting requirements under Articles 19a and 29a of Directive 2013/34/EU report on the matters covered by this Directive by publishing on their website an annual statement in a language customary in the sphere of international business. The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

In the adopted directive (Document 3), Article 16 (Communicating):

"1. Without prejudice to the exemption provided for in paragraph 2 of this Article, Member States shall ensure that companies report on the matters covered by this Directive by publishing on their website an annual statement. That annual statement shall be published:

(a) in at least one of the official languages of the Union used in the Member State of the supervisory authority designated pursuant to Article 24 and, where different, in a language that is customary in the sphere of international business;

(b) within a reasonable period of time, but no later than 12 months after the balance sheet date of the financial year for which the statement is drawn up, or, for companies voluntarily reporting in accordance with Directive 2013/34/EU, by the date of publication of the annual financial statements.

In the case of a company formed in accordance with the law of a third country, the statement shall also include the information required pursuant to Article 23(2) regarding the company's authorised representative.

2. Paragraph 1 of this Article shall not apply to companies that are subject to sustainability reporting requirements in accordance with Article 19a, 29a or 40a of Directive 2013/34/EU, including those that are exempted in accordance with Article 19a(9) or Article 29a(8) of that Directive."

Also, Article 17 mentions: "From 1 January 2029, Member States shall ensure that, when making public the annual statement referred to in Article 16(1) of this Directive, companies submit that statement at the same time to the collection body referred to in paragraph 3 of this Article for the purpose of making it accessible on the European single access point (ESAP)..."

But more importantly, Article 37 specifies when the reporting obligation (Article 16) applies for different categories of companies.

Let's focus on Article 37(a) and (b) since our company is an EU company (formed in accordance with legislation of a Member State) with 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion turnover.

Article 37(a) applies to companies with "more than 5 000 employees on average and generated a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 1 500 000 000"

Article 37(b) applies to companies with "more than 3 000 employees on average and generated a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 900 000 000"

Our company has 6,000 employees (>5,000) but EUR 1.2 billion turnover (<1.5 billion). So it doesn't meet the turnover threshold for 37(a), but it does meet the criteria for 37(b) (6,000 > 3,000 employees and 1.2 billion > 900 million turnover).

So our company falls under Article 37(b): "from 26 July 2028 as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), points (a) and (b), which are formed in accordance with the legislation of the Member State and that had more than 3 000 employees on average and generated a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 900 000 000 in the last financial year preceding 26 July 2028 for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted, with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029;"

This is crucial: "with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029"

Article 16 is the reporting/communicating requirement. So for our company, the reporting obligation under Article 16 applies for financial years starting on or after January 1, 2029.

The company's fiscal year ends June 30 (accounts closed each 30 June). So its financial years are July 1 - June 30.

The first financial year starting on or after January 1, 2029 would be the financial year starting July 1, 2029 (since July 1, 2028 starts before January 1, 2029).

Let me confirm: Financial year 2028-2029: July 1, 2028 - June 30, 2029 (starts before Jan 1, 2029)
Financial year 2029-2030: July 1, 2029 - June 30, 2030 (starts after Jan 1, 2029)

So the first financial year covered by the reporting obligation is 2029-2030 (July 1, 2029 - June 30, 2030).

Now, when is the statement for this financial year due? Article 16(1)(b) says: "within a reasonable period of time, but no later than 12 months after the balance sheet date of the financial year for which the statement is drawn up"

Balance sheet date is June 30 (end of financial year). So 12 months after June 30, 2030 would be June 30, 2031.

But let's check if there's a more specific deadline. In the proposal, it was "by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year." But in the adopted directive, it's "no later than 12 months after the balance sheet date".

Since the balance sheet date is June 30, 2030, the deadline would be June 30, 2031.

Actually, let me double-check Article 16(1)(b): "within a reasonable period of time, but no later than 12 months after the balance sheet date of the financial year for which the statement is drawn up, or, for companies voluntarily reporting in accordance with Directive 2013/34/EU, by the date of publication of the annual financial statements."

Since our company has 6,000 employees, it's likely subject to the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive (CSRD) which amended Directive 2013/34/EU, so it might fall under the second option. But the question doesn't specify, so I'll assume the general rule: 12 months after balance sheet date.

Balance sheet date: June 30, 2030
Deadline: June 30, 2031

But wait, the question asks about "the first accounting year its directive statement must cover end". The accounting year ends June 30, 2030 (for the 2029-2030 financial year).

Now, what about the Commission's proposal? When would the first statement have been due under the proposal?

The proposal (Document 1) was dated 23.2.2022. Article 30 says Member States shall apply provisions "from… [OJ to insert: 2 years from the entry into force of this Directive] as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), point (a)..."

Article 31: "This Directive shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union."

But the proposal never entered into force as proposed; it was modified and adopted as Directive 2024/1760. The question says: "Had the Commission's proposal entered into force when the adopted directive did..."

The adopted directive (Directive 2024/1760) was published in the OJ on 5.7.2024 (from the header: "5.7.2024" and "OJ L, 2024/1760, 5.7.2024").

Article 38 of adopted directive: "This Directive shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union."

So entry into force: July 5, 2024 + 20 days = July 25, 2024.

But the question says: "Had the Commission's proposal entered into force when the adopted directive did..."

So we assume the proposal entered into force on July 25, 2024 (20 days after July 5, 2024 publication).

Under the proposal, for companies under Article 2(1)(a) (which our company is), Member States shall apply provisions "from… [2 years from entry into force]".

So application date for our company under proposal: July 25, 2024 + 2 years = July 25, 2026.

But when is the first statement due? Article 11 of proposal: "The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

This is a bit ambiguous. Does "previous calendar year" mean the calendar year preceding the publication year? Or the company's financial year?

Given that companies have different financial years, but the proposal says "calendar year", it probably means the calendar year (Jan 1 - Dec 31).

But our company has a financial year ending June 30 (July 1 - June 30).

Let's read carefully: "report on the matters covered by this Directive by publishing on their website an annual statement in a language customary in the sphere of international business. The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

"Previous calendar year" likely means the calendar year that just ended. So published by April 30, 2027 would cover calendar year 2026 (Jan 1, 2026 - Dec 31, 2026).

But when does the obligation start? The provisions apply from July 25, 2026 (2 years after entry into force).

The first full calendar year after application date? Or the calendar year in which application date falls?

Typically, such obligations apply for the first full reporting period after the application date.

Application date: July 25, 2026.

First full calendar year after that would be 2027 (Jan 1, 2027 - Dec 31, 2027), with statement due by April 30, 2028.

But let's think. If the directive applies from July 25, 2026, then for the calendar year 2026 (which is partially before and partially after application date), would they need to report?

The proposal says: "The statement shall be published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

1. Under the Commission's proposal (which was Document 1)
2. Under the adopted directive (which is Document 3)

The question states: "Had the Commission's proposal entered into force when the adopted directive did, how many months after its first statement deadline under the proposal does the first accounting year its directive statement must cover end?"

Let me break this down:

First, I need to find:
1. When the adopted directive (Document 3) enters into force
2. When the first statement deadline would be under the adopted directive for this specific company
3. When the first statement deadline would have been under the Commission's proposal (Document 1) if it had entered into force at the same time as the adopted directive
4. The end of the first accounting year that the directive statement must cover
5. Calculate the difference in months between the proposal's first statement deadline and the end of that first accounting year

Let me start by finding when the adopted directive enters into force.

From Document 3 (the adopted directive), Article 38 states:
"This Directive shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union."

And from the header of Document 3:
"|Official Journal of the European Union|EN L series|
|---|---|

|2024/1760|5.7.2024|"

So it was published on July 5, 2024. It would enter into force 20 days after that, which would be July 25, 2024.

Now, Article 37 (Transposition) tells us when companies must start applying the directive:

"1. Member States shall adopt and publish, by 26 July 2026, the laws, regulations and administrative provisions necessary to comply with this Directive. They shall forthwith communicate the text of those measures to the Commission.

They shall apply those measures:
(a) from 26 July 2027 as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), points (a) and (b), which are formed in accordance with the legislation of the Member State and that had more than 5 000 employees on average and generated a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 1 500 000 000 in the last financial year preceding 26 July 2027 for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted, with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2028;"

Let me check if our company falls under this category.

The company has:
- 6,000 employees (more than 5,000)
- EUR 1.2 billion worldwide turnover (less than EUR 1.5 billion)

"(b) from 26 July 2028 as regards companies referred to in Article 2(1), points (a) and (b), which are formed in accordance with the legislation of the Member State and that had more than 3 000 employees on average and generated a net worldwide turnover of more than EUR 900 000 000 in the last financial year preceding 26 July 2028 for which annual financial statements have been or should have been adopted, with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029;"

Our company has:
- 6,000 employees (more than 3,000)
- EUR 1.2 billion worldwide turnover (more than EUR 900 million)

So it falls under category (b). The measures apply from July 26, 2028, except for Article 16 (reporting) which applies for financial years starting on or after January 1, 2029.

Now, Article 16 is about "Communicating" - the reporting requirement. Let me check Article 16:

"1. Without prejudice to the exemption provided for in paragraph 2 of this Article, Member States shall ensure that companies report on the matters covered by this Directive by publishing on their website an annual statement. That annual statement shall be published:
(a) in at least one of the official languages of the Union used in the Member State of the supervisory authority designated pursuant to Article 24 and, where different, in a language that is customary in the sphere of international business;
(b) within a reasonable period of time, but no later than 12 months after the balance sheet date of the financial year for which the statement is drawn up, or, for companies voluntarily reporting in accordance with Directive 2013/34/EU, by the date of publication of the annual financial statements.

In the case of a company formed in accordance with the law of a third country, the statement shall also include the information required pursuant to Article 23(2) regarding the company's authorised representative.

2. Paragraph 1 of this Article shall not apply to companies that are subject to sustainability reporting requirements in accordance with Article 19a, 29a or 40a of Directive 2013/34/EU, including those that are exempted in accordance with Article 19a(9) or Article 29a(8) of that Directive."

And Article 37(b) says: "with the exception of the measures necessary to comply with Article 16, which Member States shall apply to those companies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029;"

So for our company, the reporting requirement (Article 16) applies for financial years starting on or after January 1, 2029.

The company has "accounts closed each 30 June", meaning their financial year ends June 30.

So the first financial year starting on or after January 1, 2029 would be the year starting July 1, 2029 (since their year ends June 30, it starts July 1).

This financial year would end June 30, 2030.

The statement must be published "no later than 12 months after the balance sheet date", so by June 30, 2031.

Full answer
-----------

Looking at this problem, I need to:

1. Determine the first statement deadline under the Commission's proposal
2. Determine when the first accounting year under the adopted directive ends
3. Calculate the months between these two dates

Step 1: Entry into Force

The adopted directive (Document 3) was published 5.7.2024. Per Article 38, it entered into force on 25 July 2024 (twentieth day following publication). I assume the proposal would have the same entry into force date.

Step 2: Company's Status Under the Proposal

The company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion worldwide turnover, placing it under Article 2(1)(a) of the proposal (>500 employees AND >EUR 150 million turnover).

Per Article 30(1)(a) of the proposal: application date is 2 years from entry into force = 25 July 2026.

Article 11 of the proposal requires publication "by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year."

First statement deadline under the proposal: 30 April 2027

Step 3: Company's Status Under the Adopted Directive

The company has 6,000 employees (>5,000) but only EUR 1.2 billion turnover (<EUR 1.5 billion), so it does NOT qualify for Article 37(1)(a).

It does qualify for Article 37(1)(b): >3,000 employees AND >EUR 900 million turnover.

Per Article 37(1)(b): application from 26 July 2028, with Article 16 (reporting) applying "for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029."

Step 4: First Accounting Year Under the Directive

The company's fiscal year is 1 July to 30 June (closing 30 June). Financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029:

- FY 2028/29: 1 July 2028 – 30 June 2029 (starts before 1 January 2029) ✗
- FY 2029/30: 1 July 2029 – 30 June 2030 (starts on or after 1 January 2029) ✓

First accounting year the directive statement must cover ends: 30 June 2030

Step 5: Calculate the Difference

From 30 April 2027 to 30 June 2030:

- 30 April 2027 → 30 April 2030 = 36 months (3 years)
- 30 April 2030 → 30 June 2030 = 2 months

Total: 38 months

Does it meet the criteria for Article 37(1)(b)? That requires > 3,000 employees AND > EUR 900 million turnover. Our company has 6,000 employees (>3,000) and EUR 1.2 billion turnover (>EUR 900 million). So yes.

Final answer: In the original proposal the corresponding articles (e.g., on stakeholder consultation, general guidelines and climate plans) merely allowed the Commission to provide such guidance; the final directive mandates it (mainly in Article 19 and Article 22), with issuance deadlines (notably by 26 January 2027 for the due-diligence guidelines). | Your answer (gold): Guidelines on fulfilling due diligence obligations, guidance on the fitness of industry or multi-stakeholder initiatives

Full answer
-----------

The adopted CSDDD turns several discretionary “may issue guidance” provisions of the 2022 proposal into binding “shall issue” obligations. In particular, the Commission is now required to issue:

- General practical guidance on carrying out due diligence (including how to identify and prioritise adverse impacts, adapt purchasing/production practices and business strategies, and conduct meaningful stakeholder engagement);
- Sector-specific guidelines (for high-risk sectors and geographic contexts); and
- Guidance on climate-transition plans (on setting targets, decarbonisation levers and the practical implementation of the Article 22 plan).

In the original proposal the corresponding articles (e.g., on stakeholder consultation, general guidelines and climate plans) merely allowed the Commission to provide such guidance; the final directive mandates it (mainly in Article 19 and Article 22), with issuance deadlines (notably by 26 January 2027 for the due-diligence guidelines).

Full answer

In the original proposal the Commission was only permitted (“may”) to issue:

- general/sector-specific due-diligence guidelines (proposed Art. 13); and
- guidance on assessing industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives (proposed Art. 14(4)).

The adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760 turns these into obligations (“shall”) and adds one more in the same supporting-measures framework:

1. Due-diligence guidelines – Art. 19 requires the Commission to issue guidelines (including general and sector-specific or adverse-impact-specific ones, practical guidance on the climate-transition plan, risk-factor assessment, data/digital-tool references, stakeholder-engagement information, etc.).
2. Guidance on industry/multi-stakeholder initiatives – Art. 20(4) requires guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess such initiatives.
3. Guidance on third-party verification – Art. 20(5) requires guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for assessing independent third-party verifiers, plus guidance on monitoring the accuracy, effectiveness and integrity of that verification.

(By contrast, the model-contract-clause guidance was already “shall” in the proposal (Art. 12 → adopted Art. 18), so it is not part of the change.)

Full answer
-----------

The original proposal only allowed the Commission to issue guidance in two places, but the adopted Directive requires it in both.

1. General and sector-specific due-diligence guidelines

- Original proposal (Art. 13): the Commission “may issue guidelines” (including for specific sectors or adverse impacts).
- Adopted Directive (Art. 19): the Commission “shall issue guidelines” covering all of the following:
- guidance and best practices on carrying out due diligence (identification, prioritisation, appropriate measures, responsible disengagement, remediation, and stakeholder engagement/complaints);
- practical guidance on the climate-transition plan (Art. 22);
- sector-specific guidance;
- guidance on assessing risk factors (company-level, geographic/contextual, product/service, sectoral, and conflict-affected/high-risk areas);
- references to data/information sources and digital tools;
- information on how companies may share resources/information for compliance (respecting trade secrets and anti-retaliation safeguards); and
- information for stakeholders on how to engage in the due-diligence process.

(Deadlines: the (a), (d) and (e) items by 26 January 2027; the (b), (f) and (g) items by 26 July 2027.)

2. Fitness criteria for industry/multi-stakeholder initiatives

- Original proposal (Art. 14(4)): the Commission “may issue guidance” for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives.
- Adopted Directive (Art. 20(4)): the Commission, with Member States, “shall issue guidance” setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess such initiatives.

So the adopted Directive turns those two permissive provisions into mandatory ones, specifying the content listed above. (By contrast, the model contractual-clause guidance was already mandatory in the original proposal’s Art. 12 and remains so in adopted Art. 18, so it does not fit the “merely allowed → required” pattern.)

Passages quoted:

- Quoted Doc 3: “shall issue guidelines”
- Quoted Doc 1: “shall adopt guidance”

Full answer
-----------

In the Commission’s original proposal, Article 13 said the Commission “may issue guidelines” (including for specific sectors or specific adverse impacts). In the adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760, Article 19(1) makes this mandatory: the Commission “shall issue guidelines” — specifically general guidelines and sector-specific guidelines or guidelines for specific adverse impacts.

Under Article 19(2), those required guidelines must include:

- guidance and best practices on how to conduct due diligence
- practical guidance on the transition plan for climate change mitigation
- sector-specific guidance
- guidance on assessing company-level, geographic, contextual, product/service and sectoral risk factors (including conflict-affected/high-risk areas)
- references to data and information sources for compliance
- information on how to share resources and information within groups/with other entities
- information for stakeholders and their representatives on how to engage in the due diligence process

So the key change is that the guidelines under Article 19 (general, sector-specific, and adverse-impact-specific) are now obligatory, whereas the proposal only permitted them. (The proposal’s “shall adopt guidance” on model contractual clauses in Article 12 remains mandatory in adopted Article 18, and the proposal’s optional guidance on industry schemes in Article 14(4) remains optional in adopted Article 20(4).)

Reasoning
---------

Final answer: So the change from permissive to mandatory applies to these guidelines (general, sector‑specific and adverse‑impact‑specific), now with binding publication deadlines (by 26 January 2027 and 26 July 2027). | Your answer (gold): Guidelines on fulfilling due diligence obligations, guidance on the fitness of industry or multi-stakeholder initiatives

Passages quoted:

- Quoted Doc 1: “the Commission shall adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses”

Named Doc 3, Doc 1

Full answer
-----------

In the Commission’s proposal only Article 13 said the Commission “may issue guidelines” (for specific sectors or adverse impacts). In the adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760 that has become a mandatory “shall issue guidelines” duty in Article 19, with the Commission required to provide:

- guidance and best practices on carrying out due diligence (identification, prioritisation, adapting purchasing practices, responsible disengagement, remediation, and stakeholder engagement);
- practical guidance on the climate‑change‑mitigation transition plan;
- sector‑specific guidance;
- guidance on assessing risk factors (company‑level, geographic/contextual, product/service and sectoral risks, including conflict‑affected/high‑risk areas);
- references to data/information sources and digital tools/technologies;
- information on how companies may share resources and information; and
- information for stakeholders and their representatives on how to engage throughout the due diligence process.

So the change from permissive to mandatory applies to these guidelines (general, sector‑specific and adverse‑impact‑specific), now with binding publication deadlines (by 26 January 2027 and 26 July 2027).

Docs: Doc 1, Doc 2, Doc 3 · Note: —

Final answer: So the Commission is now required to issue these two sets of guidance, whereas the original proposal merely allowed it to do so. | Your answer (gold): Guidelines on fulfilling due diligence obligations, guidance on the fitness of industry or multi-stakeholder initiatives

Passages quoted:

- Quoted Doc 1: “may issue guidelines”
- Quoted Doc 1: “the Commission shall adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses”
- Quoted Doc 1: “Model contractual clauses”

Named Doc 1, Doc 2, Doc 3

Full answer
-----------

The adopted directive turns two “may issue” provisions of the proposal into “shall issue” obligations:

- Due-diligence guidelines – general, sector-specific and practical guidance on carrying out due diligence (including on risk factors, transition plans, stakeholder engagement, etc.) (Article 19 of the Directive; Article 13 of the proposal said the Commission “may issue guidelines”).
- Fitness-criteria guidance for industry/multi-stakeholder initiatives – guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess such initiatives (Article 20(4) of the Directive; Article 14(4) of the proposal said the Commission “may issue guidance” on this).

So the Commission is now required to issue these two sets of guidance, whereas the original proposal merely allowed it to do so.

Reasoning
---------

We need parse input documents: Document 1 original proposal COM(2022) 71 final, Document 2 staff working document, Document 3 adopted directive (EU) 2024/1760.

Items Where the Adopted Directive Changes "May" to "Shall"

Comparing the Commission's original proposal (COM(2022) 71 final) with the adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760, two guidance items were elevated from permissive ("may") to mandatory ("shall"):

1. Guidelines on how companies should fulfil their due diligence obligations (including general, sector-specific, and impact-specific guidelines). The original proposal (Article 13) stated the Commission "may issue guidelines." The adopted directive (Article 19) states the Commission "shall issue guidelines."
2. Guidance on assessing the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives. The original proposal (Article 14(4)) stated the Commission "may issue guidance for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives." The adopted directive (Article 20(4)) states the Commission "shall issue guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives."

Reasoning
---------

Let me analyze the question carefully. The question asks: Which guidance does the adopted directive REQUIRE the Commission to issue, where the original proposal merely ALLOWED it to? I need to compare the Commission's original proposal (Document 1) with the adopted directive (Document 3), specifically looking at provisions where the original proposal used permissive language ("may") for Commission guidance, and the adopted directive changed this to mandatory language ("shall"). Let me go through each relevant provision: **1. Model contractual clauses (Article 12 in proposal / Article 18 in adopted directive)** - Original proposal (Article 12): "the Commission **shall** adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses." - Wait, actually the original proposal already says "shall" here. Let me re-read. Original proposal Article 12: "In order to provide support to companies to facilitate their compliance with Article 7(2), point (b), and Article 8(3), point (c), the Commission **shall** adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses." - Adopted directive Article 18: "the Commission... **shall** adopt guidance about voluntary model contractual clauses, by 26 January 2027." Both are "shall" so this doesn't meet the criteria.

**2. Guidelines (Article 13 in proposal / Article 19 in adopted directive)**
- Original proposal (Article 13): "the Commission... **may** issue guidelines, including for specific sectors or specific adverse impacts."
- Adopted directive (Article 19): "the Commission... **shall** issue guidelines, including general guidelines and sector-specific guidelines or guidelines for specific adverse impacts."

This changes from "may" to "shall" — so guidelines is one item that meets the criteria.

**3. Guidance on fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives**
- Original proposal (Article 14(4)): "The Commission, in collaboration with Member States, **may** issue guidance for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives."
- Adopted directive (Article 20(4)): "The Commission, in collaboration with Member States, **shall** issue guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives."

This changes from "may" to "shall" — so this is another item.

**4. Guidance on fitness of third-party verifiers**
- Original proposal (Article 14(4)) didn't have a specific provision about third-party verifier guidance. Let me check...
- Original proposal (Article 14(4)): only mentions industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives.
- Adopted directive (Article 20(5)): "The Commission, in collaboration with Member States, **shall** issue guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of third-party verifiers, and guidance for monitoring the accuracy, effectiveness and integrity of third-party verification."

Wait, the original proposal didn't have a provision about third-party verifier guidance at all (it only mentioned "suitable industry initiatives or independent third-party verification" in the context of verifying compliance). So this is new in the adopted directive - it wasn't in the original proposal as either "may" or "shall". The question asks specifically about items where the original proposal "merely allowed it to" (i.e., used permissive language). If the provision didn't exist in the original proposal, it doesn't meet the condition. Let me re-read the question: "Which guidance, if any, does the adopted due diligence directive require the Commission to issue where the articles of the Commission's original proposal had merely allowed it to?" So the condition is: (1) adopted directive requires ("shall"), AND (2) original proposal merely allowed ("may"). If the original proposal didn't mention it at all, it doesn't meet the condition.

Let me check the original proposal again for third-party verifier guidance... The original proposal (Article 14) mentions "independent third-party verification" in the context of verifying compliance (Articles 7(4) and 8(5)), but the accompanying measure provision (Article 14) only mentions guidance for "assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives." So the guidance on third-party verifiers was NOT in the original proposal as a "may" provision - it's entirely new. So this doesn't meet the criteria.

Wait, let me re-read the original proposal more carefully. Original Article 14(4): "The Commission and the Member States may facilitate the dissemination of information on such schemes or initiatives and their outcome. The Commission, in collaboration with Member States, may issue guidance for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives." No mention of third-party verifier guidance in the original.

So third-party verifier guidance is new and doesn't meet the "merely allowed" condition.

**5. Delegated acts on reporting content (Article 11 / Article 16)**
- Original proposal (Article 11(2)): "The Commission shall adopt delegated acts in accordance with Article 28 concerning the content and criteria for such reporting under paragraph 1..." - this is "shall" in both.
- Adopted directive (Article 16(3)): "By 31 March 2027, the Commission shall adopt delegated acts..." - also "shall".

Both are "shall", so this doesn't meet the criteria.

**6. Digital tools guidance**
- Adopted directive (Article 19(2), point (e)): references to digital tools... but this is part of the general guidelines under Article 19. In the original proposal, guidelines were "may" and in the adopted directive they are "shall". So this is encompassed in the "guidelines" item.

Let me also check for the single helpdesk:
- Original proposal: didn't have a single helpdesk provision
- Adopted directive (Article 21): "The Commission shall establish a single helpdesk..." - this is new, not a change from "may" to "shall"

Now let me also check about the guidance on model contractual clauses more carefully:
- Original proposal (Article 12): "the Commission **shall** adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses." - Actually wait, let me re-read... "In order to provide support to companies to facilitate their compliance with Article 7(2), point (b), and Article 8(3), point (c), the Commission shall adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses." - Yes, it's "shall" in the original.
- Adopted directive (Article 18): "the Commission, in consultation with Member States and stakeholders, shall adopt guidance about voluntary model contractual clauses, by 26 January 2027." - "shall" in adopted.

Both are "shall". Doesn't meet criteria.

Let me check the EXPLANATORY MEMORANDUM for the original proposal about guidelines:
"In order to provide support to companies or to Member State authorities on how companies should fulfil their due diligence obligations, the Commission, where necessary in consultation with relevant European bodies, international bodies having expertise in due diligence implementation, and others, **may** issue guidelines."

And for model contractual clauses: "In order to provide support to companies to facilitate their compliance with Article 7(2), point (b), and Article 8(3), point (c), the Commission **shall** adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses."

Hmm wait, let me re-check the original proposal Article 12:

Actually I see that the explanatory memorandum says: "Article 12 sets out the obligation for the Commission to adopt guidance about non-binding model contract clauses" - so it's already mandatory in the original.

OK so let me compile the list of items where original was "may" and adopted is "shall":

1. **Guidelines** - Original Article 13: "may issue guidelines" → Adopted Article 19: "shall issue guidelines"

2. **Guidance on assessing the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives** - Original Article 14(4): "may issue guidance for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives" → Adopted Article 20(4): "shall issue guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives"

Are there any others? Let me check once more...

Original Article 13: "the Commission... may issue guidelines, including for specific sectors or specific adverse impacts."
Adopted Article 19: "the Commission... shall issue guidelines, including general guidelines and sector-specific guidelines or guidelines for specific adverse impacts."

The adopted directive also has specific sub-requirements in Article 19(2) about what guidelines shall include, and timelines in Article 19(3). But the key change is from "may" to "shall" for the guidelines.

For industry/multi-stakeholder initiatives guidance:
Original Article 14(4): "The Commission, in collaboration with Member States, may issue guidance for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives."
Adopted Article 20(4): "The Commission, in collaboration with Member States, shall issue guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives."

That's two items.

Wait, is there anything else? Let me check the original proposal's accompanying measures more carefully...

Original Article 14(3): "The Commission **may** complement Member States' support measures..." - this is about support measures, not guidance per se. In the adopted directive, Article 20(3): "The Commission **may** complement Member State support measures..." - still "may". So no change.

What about the guidance on model contractual clauses? Let me check again...

Original proposal Article 12: "In order to provide support to companies to facilitate their compliance with Article 7(2), point (b), and Article 8(3), point (c), the Commission shall adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses."

Hmm, but wait - in the adopted directive, Article 18 adds a deadline ("by 26 January 2027") and adds "in consultation with Member States and stakeholders." But the requirement itself was already mandatory.

What about guidance on climate change / transition plan?
- Original proposal Article 15: companies should adopt a plan... but no specific guidance provision.
- Adopted directive Article 19(2)(b): "practical guidance on the transition plan as referred to in Article 22" - this is part of the guidelines under Article 19, which changed from "may" to "shall".

But this is encompassed within the "guidelines" item.

What about guidance on digital tools?
- Original proposal: not mentioned
- Adopted directive Article 19(2)(e): "references to data and information sources... and to digital tools and technologies" - part of the guidelines under Article 19.

Again, encompassed within the "guidelines" item.

So the final answer is:

1. **Guidelines** (on how companies should fulfil their due diligence obligations) - changed from "may issue guidelines" to "shall issue guidelines"
2. **Guidance on assessing the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives** - changed from "may issue guidance" to "shall issue guidance"

In the adopted directive, Article 20(5) says "shall issue guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of third-party verifiers, and guidance for monitoring the accuracy, effectiveness and integrity of third-party verification." But since the original proposal didn't have a "may" provision for this, it doesn't meet the condition.

Hmm, but wait. Could one argue that the original proposal's guidance provision (Article 13 - "may issue guidelines") covered this and it was included in the general guidelines "may" provision? Let me think...

Items Changed from "May" to "Shall" (Optional → Required)

Comparing the Commission's original proposal (COM(2022) 71 final) with the adopted Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive, the following guidance items were upgraded from permissive ("may issue") to mandatory ("shall issue"):

1. Guidelines on due diligence obligations (proposal Article 13 — guidance for companies and Member State authorities on fulfilling due diligence obligations, including for specific sectors or specific adverse impacts): The proposal said the Commission "may issue guidelines." The adopted directive requires the Commission to issue these guidelines.
2. Guidance on assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives (proposal Article 14(4)): The proposal said the Commission, in collaboration with Member States, "may issue guidance for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives." The adopted directive requires the Commission to issue this guidance.

Note: The provision on model contractual clauses (proposal Article 12) already used "shall" in the proposal and is therefore not included, as it was mandatory from the outset rather than merely permitted.

Reasoning
---------

The question asks: "Which guidance, if any, does the adopted due diligence directive require the Commission to issue where the articles of the Commission's original proposal had merely allowed it to?"

So I need to identify cases where the proposal (Document 1) says the Commission "may" issue something, but the adopted directive requires it (i.e., uses "shall").

Let me go through the proposal carefully:

Article 12: "In order to provide support to companies to facilitate their compliance with Article 7(2), point (b), and Article 8(3), point (c), the Commission **shall** adopt guidance about voluntary model contract clauses."

Wait, the proposal already says "shall" here. So this is already mandatory in the proposal.

Article 13: "In order to provide support to companies or to Member State authorities on how companies should fulfil their due diligence obligations, the Commission, in consultation with Member States and stakeholders, the European Union Agency for Fundamental Rights, the European Environment Agency, and where appropriate with international bodies having expertise in due diligence, **may** issue guidelines, including for specific sectors or specific adverse impacts."

This says "may" in the proposal.

Article 14(4): "The Commission and the Member States **may** facilitate the dissemination of information on such schemes or initiatives and their outcome. The Commission, in collaboration with Member States, **may** issue guidance for assessing the fitness of industry schemes and multi-stakeholder initiatives."

These say "may" in the proposal.

1. Model contractual clauses guidance (Article 18) — The Commission shall adopt guidance on voluntary model contractual clauses.
2. General and sector-specific guidelines, including guidelines for specific adverse impacts (Article 19) — The Commission shall issue these guidelines.

4. Fitness criteria and methodology for third-party verifiers, and guidance for monitoring the accuracy, effectiveness and integrity of third-party verification (Article 20(5)) — The Commission shall issue this guidance.

Full answer
-----------

Guidance Mandated in the Adopted Directive Where the Original Proposal Was Merely Permissive

Comparing the adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760 (Document 2) with the Commission's original proposal COM(2022) 71 (as characterised in the impact assessment, Document 1), the following items are now required ("shall issue") in the adopted text where the original proposal only allowed ("may issue") the Commission to provide them:

1. Guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of industry and multi-stakeholder initiatives (Article 20(4))
2. Guidance setting out fitness criteria and a methodology for companies to assess the fitness of third-party verifiers (Article 20(5))
3. Guidance for monitoring the accuracy, effectiveness and integrity of third-party verification (Article 20(5))

These provisions sit in Article 20 ("Accompanying measures"), where the accompanying paragraph on complementary Commission support (Art. 20(3)) still uses permissive language ("may"). By contrast, the fitness-criteria and monitoring guidance was upgraded to a binding obligation ("shall issue") in the final adopted directive. The other guidance mandates (model contractual clauses in Art. 18; general and sector-specific guidelines in Art. 19) were already mandatory in the original proposal and thus do not meet the condition in the question.

Full answer
-----------

The company falls in the first tier under the 2022 proposal (>500 employees, >€150M) so obligations would start 2 years after entry into force (25 July 2024) → 25 July 2026. Its first affected July–June year is 2026–2027 (ending 30 June 2027), with the statement due within 12 months → deadline 30 June 2028.

Under the adopted CSDDD it is in the second tier (>3,000 employees, >€900M) so obligations start 26 July 2028. Its first affected July–June year is 2028–2029, ending 30 June 2029.

30 June 2029 is exactly 12 months after 30 June 2028.

Answer: 12 months.

Answer: 26 months (the first covered financial year ends on 30 June 2029, which is 26 months after the proposal’s first deadline of 30 April 2027).

Reasoning step by step

1. Entry-into-force date of the adopted Directive

- Published in the OJ on 5 July 2024 (Document 3, Art. 38) → enters into force on the 20th day ⇒ 25 July 2024.

1. When would the proposal have applied to this company?

- The company is an EU company with 6,000 employees and €1.2 bn turnover, so it falls under Art. 2(1)(a) of the proposal (Document 1: >500 employees and >€150 m turnover).
- Art. 30 of the proposal applies those provisions 2 years after entry into force ⇒ 25 July 2026.

1. First statement deadline under the proposal

- Art. 11 (Document 1) requires companies not covered by Directive 2013/34/EU to publish an annual statement by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year.
- The first calendar year of application is 2026 (from 25 July 2026) ⇒ first statement due by 30 April 2027.

1. When does the adopted Directive apply to this company?

- In the adopted Directive (Document 3, Art. 2(1)(a)) the threshold is >1,000 employees and >€450 m turnover, so the company is in scope.
- Art. 37(1)(b) applies the rules from 26 July 2028 to companies with >3,000 employees and >€900 m turnover (this company has 6,000 and €1.2 bn).

1. First financial year the directive statement must cover

- The company’s financial year runs July–June.
- Obligations start on 26 July 2028, so the first financial year in which they apply is 1 July 2028 – 30 June 2029.
- Art. 16 (Document 3) ties the statement to the financial year (published within 12 months after the balance-sheet date). Hence the first financial year covered ends on 30 June 2029.

1. Calculate the gap

- From 30 April 2027 to 30 April 2029 = 24 months.
- From 30 April 2029 to 30 June 2029 = +2 months.
- Total = 26 months.

So, under the hypothetical proposal timeline, the company’s first statement deadline would be 30 April 2027, and the first financial year its statement under the actual Directive must cover would end 26 months later, on 30 June 2029.

Final answer: Therefore, the end of the first directive-covered financial year (30 June 2030) comes 38 months after the company’s first statement deadline under the proposal (30 April 2027). | Your answer (gold): 38 months

Passages quoted:

- Quoted Doc 1: “publish an annual statement on their website.”
- Quoted Doc 3: “financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029”

Named Doc 1, Doc 3

Full answer
-----------

Answer: 38 months

Reasoning

1. Entry-into-force date (both texts)

The adopted Directive was published in the OJ on 5 July 2024 and entered into force 20 days later, i.e. 25 July 2024 (Art. 38 Dir. 2024/1760). If the Commission proposal (COM(2022) 71 final) had entered into force on the same day, its transposition/application timetable in Art. 30 of the proposal would apply:

- Large companies (Art. 2(1)(a): >500 employees and >€150 M turnover) → obligations apply 2 years after entry into force = 25 July 2026.

1. First statement deadline under the proposal

The proposal’s Art. 11 requires covered companies to publish an annual statement on their website by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year. Because the obligations start on 25 July 2026, the first reportable period is calendar year 2026 (partially covered) and the first publication deadline is 30 April 2027.

1. First financial year covered by the directive’s statement

The adopted Directive (Art. 37(1)(b)) applies the core due-diligence obligations to this company (6 000 employees, €1.2 bn turnover) from 26 July 2028, but the reporting obligation in Art. 16 applies only to financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029. With a July–June financial year, the first year that starts on or after 1 January 2029 is 1 July 2029 – 30 June 2030. So the first financial year the directive’s statement must cover ends on 30 June 2030 (published no later than 12 months after the balance-sheet date, i.e. by 30 June 2031 – Art. 16(1) Dir.).

1. Time gap

From 30 April 2027 to 30 June 2030 is 3 years and 2 months = 38 months.

Therefore, the end of the first directive-covered financial year (30 June 2030) comes 38 months after the company’s first statement deadline under the proposal (30 April 2027).

Full answer

Answer: 26 months

Reasoning

1. When would the proposal have entered into force?

The adopted Directive entered into force on 25 July 2024 (published 5 July 2024 + 20 days, Art. 38 Doc 3). Assuming the Commission’s proposal (Doc 1) entered into force on the same date:

- Transposition deadline: 25 July 2026 (2 years, Art. 30(1) Doc 1)
- Application for large EU companies (Art. 2(1)(a) – >500 employees and >EUR 150 M turnover): 25 July 2026 (Art. 30(1)(a) Doc 1).

1. First statement deadline under the proposal

- Art. 11 Doc 1 requires an annual statement published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year.
- The company first becomes subject on 25 July 2026, so the first covered period is calendar year 2026.
- First deadline: 30 April 2027.

1. When do the Directive’s obligations apply to this company?

The company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 bn turnover (July–June FY).

- Under the Directive (Doc 3) it falls in Art. 2(1)(a) (>1,000 employees, >EUR 450 M).
- Art. 37 applies it in group (b): >3,000 employees and >EUR 900 M turnover → obligations start on 26 July 2028 (not group (a), because turnover is <EUR 1.5 bn).

1. First financial year covered by the Directive’s statement

- The Directive links reporting to the financial year (Art. 16(1)(b) Doc 3: within 12 months after the balance‑sheet date of the financial year for which the statement is drawn up).
- The first FY that includes 26 July 2028 is 1 July 2028 – 30 June 2029.
- So the first FY ends on 30 June 2029.

1. Calculating the gap

- Proposal first deadline: 30 April 2027
- Directive first covered FY ends: 30 June 2029

From 30 April 2027 to 30 June 2029 is 2 years and 2 months = 26 months.

Therefore, the first financial year the company’s statement under the Directive must cover ends 26 months after its first statement deadline under the proposal.

Step‑by‑step reasoning

| Issue | Proposal (COM(2022) 71) | Adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760 | |---|---|---| | Entry into force | Hypothetically the same as the Directive: published 5 July 2024 → in force 25 July 2024 (Art. 38 Directive) | 25 July 2024 | | Scope of the company | EU company, 6 000 employees, €1.2 bn worldwide turnover → falls in Art. 2(1)(a) (group 1: >500 employees & >€150 m) | Same figures → in scope of Art. 2(1)(a) (>1 000 employees & >€450 m) | | When do the obligations start? | Art. 30: provisions apply 2 years after entry into force for group 1 → 25 July 2026 | Art. 37(1)(b): applies from 26 July 2028 for companies with >3 000 employees and >€900 m turnover (the company meets the employee threshold but not the €1.5 bn threshold of point (a)) | | What must be published? | Art. 11: an annual statement, published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year | Art. 16: an annual statement covering the financial year, published no later than 12 months after the balance‑sheet date | | First publication deadline / first covered period | First deadline after obligations begin: 30 April 2027 (covering Jan–Dec 2026, the part of which falls within the scope) | Obligations begin 26 July 2028, inside the July 2028–June 2029 financial year. So the first financial year that must be covered ends on 30 June 2029 |

Calculation

- Proposal first statement deadline: 30 April 2027
- Directive first covered financial‑year end: 30 June 2029

From April 2027 to June 2029 is 2 years and 2 months = 26 months.

Full answer
-----------

Calculating the Timeline Difference

Step 1: Identify the Company's Scope Under Each Document

Under the proposal (COM(2022) 71):

- Article 2(1)(a): >500 employees AND >EUR 150M turnover → Group 1 (6,000 employees ✓, EUR 1,200M ✓)

Under the adopted CSDDD (Directive 2024/1760):

- Article 2(1)(a): >5,000 employees AND >EUR 1,500M turnover → 6,000 > 5,000 ✓ but 1,200 ≯ 1,500 ✗ → NOT Group 1
- Article 2(1)(b): >3,000 employees AND >EUR 900M turnover → 6,000 > 3,000 ✓, 1,200 > 900 ✓ → Group 2

Step 2: Determine Key Dates

Both enter into force 25 July 2024 (per the question's premise).

Under the proposal:

- Application (Group 1): 25 July 2026 (2 years after entry into force)
- Article 11: Statement published by 30 April each year, covering the previous calendar year
- First statement deadline: 30 April 2027 (covering calendar year 2026)

Under the adopted CSDDD:

- Application (Group 2): 26 July 2028
- First financial year covered (July–June company): FY July 2028 – June 2029 (the financial year containing the application date)
- End of first financial year: 30 June 2029

Step 3: Calculate the Difference

From 30 April 2027 to 30 June 2029:

- April 30, 2027 → April 30, 2028 = 12 months
- April 30, 2028 → April 30, 2029 = 12 months (running total: 24)

Full answer
-----------

Calculating the Timeline Difference

Under the Adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760

Entry into force: 25 July 2024 (20th day after publication on 5 July 2024, per Article 38).

Scope (Art. 2(1)(a)): 6,000 employees (>1,000) and EUR 1.2B turnover (>EUR 450M) → in scope ✓

Phased application (Art. 37(1)):

- (a) requires >5,000 employees and >EUR 1.5B turnover → not met (turnover only EUR 1.2B)
- (b) requires >3,000 employees and >EUR 900M turnover → met ✓ → application from 26 July 2028

Article 16 exception: reporting applies for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029.

With a July–June financial year:

- FY 2028–29 (July 2028 – June 2029) starts before 1 Jan 2029 → not covered
- FY 2029–30 (July 2029 – June 2030) starts after 1 Jan 2029 → first covered FY ✓

End of first covered FY: June 2030

Under the Commission's Proposal (COM(2022) 71), hypothetically in force 25 July 2024

The proposal provided a uniform application timeline with a 2-year transposition period (by 25 July 2026) and no phased approach. Reporting would cover financial years starting on or after the application date:

- FY 2026–27 (July 2026) starts before 25 July 2026 → not covered
- FY 2027–28 (July 2027 – June 2028) starts after 25 July 2026 → first covered FY ✓

First statement deadline: 12 months after the 30 June 2028 balance sheet date = 30 June 2029

Result

$$\text{June 2030} - \text{June 2029} = \boxed{12 \text{ months}}$$

The first financial year the company's statement under the directive must cover ends 12 months after its first statement deadline under the proposal.

Full answer
-----------

Calculating the Time Difference

Under the Adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760

Identifying the application tier (Article 37(1)):

- Article 37(1)(a): >5,000 employees AND >EUR 1.5B turnover → Company has 6,000 employees ✓ but EUR 1.2B ✗ → Does not qualify
- Article 37(1)(b): >3,000 employees AND >EUR 900M turnover → 6,000 ✓ and EUR 1.2B ✓ → Qualifies

Key dates for this tier:

- General application: from 26 July 2028
- Article 16 (communicating) measures: for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029

First covered financial year (July–June): FY starting 1 July 2029 → FY July 2029 – June 2030

End of first FY under the directive = 30 June 2030

Under the Commission's Proposal (entering into force 25 July 2024)

The proposal (COM(2022) 71) had a 2-year transposition period → application from 25 July 2026 for large companies (500+ employees, EUR 150M+ turnover — which this company exceeds).

First covered FY (July–June): the first FY starting after 25 July 2026 is FY July 2027 – June 2028 (since FY July 2026 starts before 25 July 2026).

Statement deadline: 12 months after the balance sheet date (30 June 2028) → 30 June 2029

Result

| Event | Date | |---|---| | First statement deadline under proposal | 30 June 2029 | | End of first FY under directive | 30 June 2030 |

$$30\text{ June 2030} - 30\text{ June 2029} = \boxed{12 \text{ months}}$$

Reasoning
---------

Let me analyze this step by step.

**The company:**
- EU company
- Always has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion worldwide turnover
- July-June financial year (i.e., financial year runs from July 1 to June 30)
- Must publish its due diligence statement

**Under the adopted Directive (EU) 2024/1760:**

The Directive entered into force on 25 July 2024 (20th day after publication on 5 July 2024).

Transposition: Member States adopt by 26 July 2026.

Application dates (Article 37):
- (a) From 26 July 2027: companies with more than 5,000 employees and more than EUR 1.5 billion net worldwide turnover- (b) From 26 July 2028: companies with more than 3,000 employees and more than EUR 900 million net worldwide turnover
- (c) From 26 July 2027: third-country companies with more than EUR 1.5 billion EU turnover
- (d) From 26 July 2028: third-country companies with more than EUR 900 million EU turnover
- (e) From 26 July 2029: all other companies under Article 2(1)(a) and (b), and Article 2(2)(a) and (b), and companies under Article 2(1)(c) and (2)(c)

The company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion worldwide turnover.

Under Article 2(1)(a): companies with more than 1,000 employees and more than EUR 450 million turnover → This company qualifies.

Now, which application date applies?

Article 37(1)(a): more than 5,000 employees AND more than EUR 1.5 billion turnover → No, the company has only EUR 1.2 billion (less than 1.5 billion). So not (a).

Article 37(1)(b): more than 3,000 employees AND more than EUR 900 million turnover → Yes! 6,000 > 3,000 and 1.2 billion > 900 million. So (b) applies.

From 26 July 2028: applies to this company, with the exception of Article 16 measures which apply for financial years starting on or after 1 January 2029.

Wait, but the question asks about the "due diligence statement." Under the directive, Article 16 requires an annual statement. But wait - is this company subject to sustainability reporting under Directive 2013/34/EU (Article 19a)? The company has 6,000 employees and EUR 1.2 billion turnover. Under the CSRD, companies with more than 500 employees (large undertakings) would be subject to sustainability reporting.
